DM: "Kit, the clerk and his case! Hall guard, the back door!"

Your voice cracks across the hall like a whip, and everything happens at once. Kit is moving before you finish, and she is slower than usual, since exhaustion has halved her stride as it has yours. But she doesn't need speed. The clerk has been sidling along the dais with his case hugged to his chest, and when he sees a stocky woman in a blue tabard bearing down on him he stops dead, as a rabbit does under a hawk.

You flick two fingers and a pale, shimmering hand appears by the doorframe. Mage Hand doesn't need to hit anything. It only has to push, and the door isn't locked. It swings shut in Corvin's face with a solid wooden thud, just as his gloved fingers close on the latch ring.

Then you run. With your speed halved by exhaustion it's a stiff, graceless run, but you take the low step in a clumsy hop and land at the dais's edge, five feet short of him, breathing hard. That is as far as you can get this turn. It's close enough.

Corvin stands with his hand still on the latch and his back to you. For a moment his shoulders do nothing at all. Then he turns, and the gentle teacher's mask is gone, and what's under it is only a tired, cold, calculating man.

"That was clever," he says, quietly enough that the hall can't hear. "I taught you the hand."

Behind you, the two Vantry guards are crossing the floor at a steady jog, halberds up, and the arbiter's voice rings out: "Hold that man!" Kit has the clerk by the sleeve, and the case has fallen on the floor with a clack of metal fittings. Sir Corwen is on his feet, and Isaude has stood with him.

Corvin's eyes flick to the door, then to the guards, then back to you. The latch ring is under his hand, and he could try to haul it open again. One look says he is weighing it.

The guards will reach him in about three heartbeats. You've a rapier at your hip, a dagger, one spell slot, and a few seconds. What does Wren do?

She sets the reliquary's foot in a socket at the lantern's base and turns it. Something clicks, and the lantern's glass pane swings open on a hinge you never saw.

Inside, rolled thin as a cigarette, is a strip of paper in your mother's tilted hand: *I go to the Gullspit light. If I am not back by midwinter, light the road, not sell it. Trust the lamp, not the smile.*

"That's all she left," Ysolde says quietly, "except that I was to give the staff to you and no one else." She looks at you for a long moment. "You have her chin."

The staff is yours, with the strip of paper. The Gullspit light is a name you don't know. Outside, the fog is greying toward dawn, and the dawn bell will ring in perhaps an hour. Sparrow is still standing in the lane under Kit's crossbow.

What does Wren do?

DM: You pick your dagger out of the doorway, wipe it on your sleeve and sheathe it. You lay the lamp-staff beside it, press your thumb to your forehead and say quietly, "I found it, Mother. I found the light." The wind is the only answer. Above you the lamp burns white, steady and warm as a hand.

When you ask, Brann sits on a stone and tells it all. He's slow, but he doesn't leave out what he remembers.

Elowen came to the spit alone in the autumn sixteen years ago, already fevered and thin as a lath. She said her daughter was "safe among people who will love her, far from the man who sells lanterns." She said she had hidden the child's inheritance in three places: a coffer upriver, a staff at the Wick-House, and the lamp here. In her third week a grey-sailed ship came. A hooded man landed and talked with her alone on the shingle, and Brann heard her call him "Aldous". She told him, "It is not yours to light." He left furious. She died that winter of the fever, and asked to be buried with her face to the lamp. Brann kept the light dark by her word and waited for the lantern-foot.

"I thought him dead," he says, looking out across the water at the grey sail, now very small. "He had a way of looking at the lamp that I've never seen on a man since, as if it owed him money."

You now have a name for the Grey Cloak. Aldous. It is a little past eight. The lamp is lit and cannot be put out while your lantern-foot stays seated. The cutter is a speck on the grey water, three dead men lie on the shingle, and Saltmarsh is across the estuary. You've no spell slots left, and a lot of unanswered questions.

  [game] notebook: updated The Gullspit Light
DM: Out of the story first, on both your questions.

On the name: I'll be straight with you. I picked "Aldous" for the Grey Cloak without checking it against Brother Osric's register, so it wasn't a planted clue. It does collide with Lord Edric's long-dead grandsire, and you're right to notice. I'll leave it open, as a possible lead and not a promise. Aldous Verrel would be far too old to be your Grey Cloak, so if there's a link, it's an alias or a family tie, and you'd find it by asking Lord Edric, Osric, or the Thornhallow records. It could also just be a common river-country name. Chase it if you like, and you'll learn whatever the world gives you.

On the lamp, here's my ruling, which I'll apply the same way every time. The great lamp burns only while the reliquary's lantern-foot is seated in its socket. If you take the foot, the lamp goes dark. If you put it back, it lights again. You'd need the foot to open Elowen's other hiding places, and the paper says it is the proof of your claim.

Now the story. Brann shakes his head slowly. "No surname, no house. Only 'Aldous', said the way she said it, like a thing she'd known a long time." He adds, with an effort, "The ship had a grey sail, much like that one. That's all."

Kit has finished laying out the dead. She stands up, brushing shingle off her knees, and looks at the sky, which has begun to cloud over from the west. "Weather," she says. Brann's squall is on its way.

So the lamp is lit and has to be a choice. You can leave the reliquary in the socket, so the light burns on and Brann keeps it, though you'd have no lantern-foot for any other doors. Or you can take the foot and let the lamp go dark again.

Dunn is ready to take the dead and the living back to Saltmarsh, though he mutters about the weather. What does Wren do?

Then I turn to Kit and Dunn. "Load the dead hands, let's go before the squall is on us, and Dunn, I'll add two silver if you take us home dry."

DM: Out of the story first: yes, there is word. The arbiter will tell you in the scene below. Corvin signed last night.

You wring out your cloak on the quay, meet the arbiter's eye and say it plainly. Three dead hands from the Heron's Tithe, a hooded man called Aldous, a man named Sparrow, and a request to give the full account by a fire. She doesn't hesitate. At the name *Aldous* her stylus stops, though she says nothing. "Indoors," she says. "Pell, the dead go to the dead-house, and nobody touches their pockets but me."

The elder's counting-room has a fire, a towel and hot cider with too much clove. You tell it in order, and Kit adds the dry parts. The arbiter listens without interrupting, then lays a folded sheet in front of you.

"Master Tallowmere signed before dusk," she says. "A clerk and my own guard took it down word for word, as you asked." She taps the page. "It names the last places he knew Elowen Ashgrove to have been, Ashby Weir and then a spit he never found. It names *H.* as a Master Hale, now held at Thornhallow. And it says Hale acted for a patron the prisoner knew only as *Aldous*." She looks up at you. "The patron paid for the whole trade in forged pedigrees. He wanted the ledger, and he wanted your mother's staff. Master Tallowmere says he never saw his face." She pauses. "He also says the man 'wears a dead man's name.'"

So the Aldous you heard about on the spit is the same man. Corvin's phrase fits the thing you asked me about, but it doesn't settle it. A dead man's name could mean a stolen alias, and the register Hale once tried to read could be where it came from.

The arbiter will keep the dead hands as witnesses and send a rider to Thornhallow with the name. She's ordered the harbour watch to seize the Heron's Tithe if she returns to Saltmarsh. The squall is still beating on the shutters. It is about eleven in the morning.

You have no spell slots left, you're still at exhaustion 1, and you haven't slept since dawn. Your reliquary is on the spit. What does Wren do?

DM: The arbiter doesn't ask why. She looks at the rain on the shutters and at your face, then sends Pell down with you. Pell stays at the cellar door with his halberd grounded and his eyes on the ceiling, a man who is hearing nothing.

Corvin sits on a cot with a blanket over his knees. The black gloves are gone, and his hands look older without them. He sees the folded statement and smiles, rueful and tired. "You came back. I did wonder."

You read him his own line. He doesn't flinch.

"A dead man's name." He turns it over like a coin. "I heard it once, on a wet night in a roadhouse, when he'd had too much of the good wine and thought I was too far gone to listen. He said, 'It's only borrowed, Corvin. I'm wearing it until I've earned it back.' And later: the real Aldous was 'a Verrel by blood, and no friend to the Verrels' paper.' That's all he ever gave me. I think the man he is was never meant to have a name, and he's trying on one that has a claim attached." He spreads his hands. "Whether that makes him a bastard, a cousin, or a thief, I never learned. I swear it on the Lantern, whatever you think that's worth."

You ask about yourself, and the room gets quieter.

"No," he says. "It was never the whole of it." He looks at the wall for a while. "I didn't buy you, Wren. I told the caravan I did because it made a better tale. You were four, perhaps five. Elowen had left you at a roadside shrine-house on the lower Marrow, with a woman who took coin to keep you and ask nothing. I found it. I paid the woman more than Elowen had, and I took you." His voice stays level. "I told myself it was to keep Aldous from finding you, and that was partly true. The rest is that a child with Elowen's face, kept near the lantern-foot, was worth keeping. I won't dress that up. I raised you well enough, and I used you, and both are so."

You press for the name of the shrine-house and the woman. He shakes his head slowly. "Not from this cot. Get me out of this cellar into Dame Crowe's keeping, where the road is open to me and the man who smiles can't reach me, and I'll draw you the map myself." A thin, dry smile. "I know how that sounds. Your mother's staff is in your hands, and you've still not trusted me, which is the best thing I ever taught you."

You study him as he speaks. Your Insight check is a 13 (with disadvantage from exhaustion), and he doesn't give you anything to hold. Every word sounds like the truth, and it sounds the same way his best tales always did. You can't tell which parts are true and which are his craft.

It is a little after one. The storm is still grumbling overhead, Kit is asleep upstairs, and Pell is very carefully not listening. What does Wren do?

DM: Corvin is quiet for a long moment. When he speaks, the warmth has gone out of his voice, and what's left is plainer.

"Alive, and in one piece, and under oath." He gives a short, dry laugh. "You'd have made a poor forger, Wren. You always told the truth in the wrong places." He lifts a hand toward Pell. "Guard. Paper and a pen, if the hall can spare them."

You don't turn around. Behind you the cot creaks as he settles. "In my own hand, then. The shrine-house, the woman, the year. It's in Corvin's own neat hand. He has drawn the lower Marrow as a ribbon, with Saltmarsh at its mouth and a mark a day's ride upriver on the east bank. *Reedlow Landing. The Rushlight House, a ferry-stop shrine. Goodwife Annis Marl kept the child through one winter and a spring. Say Elowen's name only after she has said yours.* Below that, in smaller writing: *She may be dead. If so, her niece keeps the ferry. I would not go by the main road.*

You try to check it against what you hold. You go through Elowen's letter and the strip from the staff, and your Investigation check is only a 4, with disadvantage from exhaustion. DM: Out of the story first, on your question. My ruling is that the day count and the calendar date match, so "month's end" is the 30th and Day 10 is the 10th. That leaves twenty days to Dame Crowe's assize. Marrowford is about four days' ride from Saltmarsh by the river road. Reedlow Landing lies a day upriver on the way, so it is on your road and you'd lose almost nothing by going. Your three-day round trip fits easily. The arbiter must still see to Corvin's own journey to the assize, but you've promised to see him there alive.

Bess has hot porridge and the grey horse Ember is saddled in the yard. The back road runs inland along the dike, longer than the main one, and Corvin warned against the main road.

"Not bad," you tell her. "Don't tell anyone," she says. It is the longest conversation she has offered in days.

You stop once to water the horses and eat bread and cheese in the lee of a dike-mill. The back road takes its time, winding around marsh and drainage ditches. The sun is low and gold when the land begins to rise toward a belt of alders and a flat brown stretch of river.

Reedlow Landing is a handful of low houses, a reed-thatched ferry shed, a stone jetty, and a flat-bottomed ferry tied against the current. A little way back from the water, on the old road, stands a small shrine-house of grey stone, plain as a cattle-shed. A tin lantern with a rush-wick hangs over its door, unlit. Two or three villagers eye you over their doors, and a woman sweeping a step stops to watch you come.

It is a little after five. The jetty and the ferry-shed are on your right, the shrine on the left, and the sun has about two hours left. You've three spell slots, three Bardic Inspirations, and Corvin's warning: *Say Elowen's name only after she has said yours.* What does Wren do?

She is broad and weathered, with forearms like a ferryman's and a face that gives nothing away. She leans the broom against the wall and looks you over, from the rapier to the lamp-staff to your face. Then she looks at your face a second time.

"Annis Marl," she says slowly, "is my aunt. She's abed, and she's old, and she doesn't take strangers." Her mouth tightens. "I'm Tamsin. I keep the ferry. Say your business plain, and then say your name, because I'll not have my aunt troubled for a name I can't trust."

You knock anyway, politely, on the plain door, and before you've finished the third rap a thin, cracked voice calls from within. "Tamsin. Who is it? Let her in. She's got a step like a girl with a lute."

Tamsin stares at the door and then at you. "She can't see a hand in front of her face these ten years," she mutters, and holds it open.

Inside is one low room, warm and dim, with a rush-light on a shelf and a little hearth. In a chair by the fire sits a tiny old woman wrapped in a patched shawl, her eyes clouded white. Her hands move on her lap as though she were spinning thread. A cat on the hearth opens one eye and shuts it again.

She tips her head toward you. "Come where I can feel you," she says. "Say a name for me, child. Not hers. Yours."

DM: You kneel by the chair, close enough that her hands can find you. Her fingers are light and dry as paper. They go over your face the way a weaver feels cloth, across the brow, down the cheek, and last to the chin. She stops there for a long moment.

"Wren," she says. It isn't a question. "Aye. She called you that, the one who brought you. I called you Mouse, because you wouldn't speak." Her clouded eyes fill, and she doesn't wipe them. "Tamsin, put the kettle on. And bar the door."

Tamsin does both, and she is gentler than she looked on the step.

Then Annis tells it, slowly. Elowen came on a cold autumn evening, fevered and thin, with a small girl asleep on her shoulder. She paid a silver coin and a promise. "I was to keep you, and ask nothing, and give you a toy, a little tin lantern, to whoever came saying her own name and not asking for another." She's quiet a while. "She came back once, at midwinter, and held you all night. Then she went away down the river road, and I never saw her face again."

In the spring a silver-haired man came with a better offer and a warm voice. "I was poor, child, and afraid, and he talked like a priest. I let him take you. I've been sorry for it every year since."

There is more. The year after, a different man came to the door, and Annis's voice drops. "Courteous. Quiet. A grey cloak, and a ring on his hand with a green stone in it that held the light. He asked after a girl, a small one, with her mother's chin. I told him you'd died of the fever and were buried in the reeds. He looked at me a long time and thanked me kindly. And then he paid for my fire-wood all that winter, which frightened me more than a blow would have."

She reaches under the shawl and draws out a little tin lantern, no bigger than a hen's egg, painted with a crooked flame and worn nearly smooth by old thumbs. It opens on a hinge. Inside is a slip of paper in your mother's hand: *She is Wren. Whoever asks for her by another name is no friend of hers.* You put it in your hand, and it fits.

"I kept it fifteen years," Annis says. "It was always yours."

You now hold the tin lantern toy, and you have a new mark for your enemy: a green-stone ring on a courteous hand. It is nearly six in the evening. Outside, the light is going orange over the river, and Kit waits with the horses. Tamsin is staring at the shutters and says quietly, "There was a rider on the dike road at noon who didn't come down. I'd thought nothing of it."

What does Wren do?

DM: You take Annis's hands in both of yours and say it plainly. She presses her lips together and nods, and a tear runs down the lines of her face. "You're kind, as she was," she whispers.

About the ring, she shakes her head. "No crest, child. A plain band, and a stone as green as a beech leaf. It caught the firelight, and I remember that." She frowns, working at the memory. "He gave no name. He said one thing, kindly, as you'd speak to a child: *her mother took something that belongs to my family.* And he thanked me and went."

Tamsin has been at the shutters. "A man in a brown cloak on a pale horse," she says. "At noon he sat up on the dike a quarter mile off for an hour, and then he went. I couldn't swear to a smile at that distance." She is already moving. She fetches Kit and the horses into the byre behind the shrine, and douses the rush-light with a pinch of her fingers.

You take the east shutter and Kit takes the door, with her shield on her arm and her crossbow across her knees. You watch the road go violet and then grey. At a little after half past six, your Perception check is a 14, and you see it. A figure in a brown cloak sits a pale horse on the crest of the dike, a quarter mile off and absolutely still against the last of the light. Then he dips below the rise and is gone. You can't say whether he has seen the dark house or the dead lamp.

Annis, in her chair by the cold hearth, says drily into the dark: "Someone's out there. The cat won't settle." Tamsin has taken down a ferry-hook as long as her arm.

It is half past six, the night is coming on, and you have three slots, three Inspirations, Kit, and a village of strangers. What does Wren do?

== ROUND 107 ==
PLAYER: I whisper to Kit and Tamsin, "He's watching a dark house, so let's make him think it's asleep." I light nothing, have us all lie low through the first half of the night, and plan a trap for the second: Kit at the byre door, Tamsin with her hook at the ferry shed, me at the east shutter with Faerie Fire ready if anyone approaches, and Annis kept safe behind the hearth.

If he sends a scout, I'll cast Faerie Fire on him in the dark so Kit can take a shot, then call out in my best fishwife voice, "Ferry's closed till morning, friend!"

A leaf-green stone in a plain band, on the master's left hand, and he turns it when he's thinking. Dace has only seen him twice, across a lamp-lit table, and never his face fully. The master calls himself the natural son of the old Aldous Verrel, "born on the wrong side of the blanket and robbed of Thornhallow." Dace says it quickly, like a story he has heard told too many times. "He says there's a leaf cut out of the chapel's register that proves it. He wears his dead father's name till he can prove it's his." He swallows. "That's all I know of that, I swear."

And then, with the same fear: his orders were to follow you from Saltmarsh and signal from the dike with a shuttered lantern, so Sparrow, who waits in a skiff at Fenny Reach, a drowned boat-yard two miles downriver, would know where you slept. If no signal came by dawn, Sparrow would come looking. "The master's orders for you," he says, very low, "are to take you and anything of your mother's alive, and leave nobody to talk after."

Behind you, Tamsin spits into the dust. Inside the house, Annis's cracked voice calls again, quiet and steady: "Tamsin. Is it done?"

We kept our lantern up, and she didn't like it." He nods at the light on the spit. "Your old man's still up there. He waved at us twice."

The sky is going pearl-grey when the skiff grounds at the weedy landing. The Gullspit light burns white above you, steady, with no scorch on the stone and no mark on the door. It is a quarter to six. Elowen told me never to open it without the foot, and I've never tried." He turns his cup in his hands. "The foot's in the lamp up there. Take it to open the door, and the light goes dark."

You've three 1st-level slots, all your Inspiration, and the morning ahead. The foot is the key to something, and the lamp is the lock the master said. Taking the foot darkens the light, and that costs you the beacon Saltmarsh has been watching for three days. Leaving it keeps the light and keeps the door shut. The guard boat will stay near until noon. What does Wren do?

  [game] notebook: updated The Gullspit Light
DM: Brann leads the way down a stair slick with old salt and holds the candle high. The cellar is low and round, floored with flagstones and ringed with rust-brown scars where seawater has stood. The iron door is set into the far wall, black and square, with no handle.

You take your time with it, with Kit at your shoulder and Brann's candle shaking the shadows. Your Investigation check is a 17, and the door gives up its secrets.

There are no needles, no runes, and no hidden catches. At the centre is a lantern-shaped plate with a socket in it, and your reliquary's foot would fit it exactly. Scratched into the iron beside it in your mother's tilted hand are two small marks: a single arrow turning left, with the words *once, for the road*, and two arrows turning right, with the words *twice, for the sea*. Whatever that means, it reads like a warning or a choice.

You also see what the cellar has been telling you. A green line of weed runs a hand's breadth above the threshold, so this chamber floods at the highest tides, and the door's lower hinge is rust-pitted but sound. The tide is climbing toward its morning high at about nine. You have three hours before the water reaches the stairs.

So the door isn't a trap, but it isn't simple either. The foot turns left once for the road, or right twice for the sea. You don't know which one opens it, and you don't know what the other does.

You have three 1st-level slots, all your Inspiration, Kit at your shoulder, Brann holding the candle, and Dunn waiting at the landing. The lamp is still burning upstairs. What does Wren do?

DM: You say the words and draw the sign in the air, and the ritual takes ten quiet minutes, long enough that Brann's candle gutters twice and Kit shifts her weight. Then the spell opens like a second sense behind your eyes.

You sense magic in two places. The lantern plate on the iron door shows a thin, steady Abjuration aura, the kind that guards. Around the door, set into the flagstones in a ring like a faint chalk line, is a second, fainter aura. It reads as Conjuration, and you have the odd idea that it is waiting. The iron door is thick enough to block the spell, so you learn nothing about what lies beyond it. You don't learn which turn is which, because the spell does not say, but the two auras fit the two warnings: one turn for a guard, one turn for the sea.

You keep the spell running and climb the stair, and Kit watches the dark behind you with her crossbow ready. In the lantern-room the great lamp turns in its ring, and the white light seems to lean toward you. You lift the reliquary's foot out of its socket and the light dies, instantly and completely. The whole tower goes cold and grey, and Brann says nothing but breathes out hard behind you. Through the cracked glass the guard boat's lantern is a small yellow star on the water, and Dunn can be seen on the landing.

The reliquary is back in your hand and in your pack. By now it is ten past six, with the tide still rising. You go back down the stair with Kit, and Detect Magic is still on you with a few minutes left. The lantern plate waits in the cellar, and so does the choice. Do you turn the foot once to the left for the road, or twice to the right for the sea? Or do you try something else first? What does Wren do?

DM: "Everyone up two steps, in case I'm wrong," you say. Kit takes Brann by the elbow and they climb, and Kit's crossbow stays on the stair. You seat the reliquary's foot in the lantern plate, it fits with a click, and you turn it once to the left, for the road.

Deep in the iron something shifts, slow and heavy, like a key in a very old lock. The ring of Conjuration in the flagstones stays quiet and dark. The door breathes out a smell of cold stone and dry paper, swings inward on a hinge that makes no sound, and does not flood the cellar.

Beyond it is a small stone cell, dry as a bone, no bigger than a cupboard. On a shelf cut into the wall is a lead-lined tube, a little brass lantern the size of a thumb, and a folded letter. The tube has a faint ward on it that your Detect Magic, still running, reads as Abjuration. Nothing else is in the room, and nothing moves.

The letter is in your mother's tilted hand:

*My wren, if the door opened to the road, then you are who I hoped, and you are kind enough not to have drowned us both. In the tube is a leaf I cut from the Thornhallow register, which an old chaplain gave me because he knew a man would one day come to burn it or to use it. One side is a baptism, Aldous Verrel the younger, a servant's child, his father consenting. The other side is the child's burial, three years on. The boy is dead, and he has been dead thirty years. A man who comes to you wearing that name wears a dead child's grave. He is not what he says. He first came to me as a patron of our shrines, and then asked for the lamp. Do not let him make you his. Burn the leaf if you must, keep it if you can, and whatever you choose, choose it as yourself. Light the road, Wren. —E.*

You read the leaf by Brann's candle: the baptism on one side, and the burial on the other, in the same cramped chaplain's hand. It matches the register copy you carry, and the note about the missing page in Brother Osric's book. It proves the Grey Cloak's story false. It also proves nothing about who he really is.

You take the leaf, the letter, and the little brass lantern (Elowen's last gift is a thumb-sized lamp with a wick the size of a whisker). You take the foot back out of the plate and shut the iron door. Ten minutes, then twenty, pass in the cellar as you read and think. It is nearly half past six in the morning, and the lamp upstairs is dark. The tide is still rising. The guard boat waits on the water, and Dunn is yawning on the landing.

You've got the proof. You've got your mother's words. The reliquary's foot goes back in the lamp if you want the light burning, or stays with you if you want to carry the key. And the Grey Cloak will come for the leaf, if he's learned it's yours. What does Wren do?

DM: Brann doesn't argue for long. He stands at the dark lamp with his hand on its cold brass, says "Sixteen years, and I never left it for a night," and then takes his patched coat and a bundle of his few things. "She would have said go," he admits.

You sit on the landing with your Forgery Kit and make the copy. Your check is a 20, so the hand, the ruling and even the chaplain's cramped abbreviations come out true. You write in the margin, in your own plain letters, *Copy, made by Wren Ashgrove, Day 13. The original is in the arbiter's keeping.* Honest forgery is a strange feeling, and you find you like it. You keep the copy and Elowen's letter. Before you leave, you and Kit wedge a driftwood spar into the iron cellar door's frame, and Brann turns the old key in the tower door and gives it to you.

Dunn takes you back across with the tide near its height. Captain Dolan raises a hand from the guard boat as you pass and then follows you in. By a little before nine you're at the Vantry hall. The arbiter reads Elowen's letter and the leaf and sets her stylus down carefully. She says nothing about the name "Aldous" for a long moment. Then she seals the original in a tube with the hall's wax, writes your name across the seal, and puts it in her own strongbox. "It goes to Dame Crowe in my hand or in Pell's, and no one else's," she says. "And I will send a rider to Thornhallow today with your news that the name is a dead child's grave. Lord Edric should hear it before anyone else does."

Brann is given a bed at the Wick-House, where Mother Ysolde takes one look at him and puts the kettle on, as if she had been expecting him for sixteen years. The Gullspit light is dark over the estuary, the first time in three days. You carry the lantern-foot, the lamp-staff, the copy and your mother's letters. You have three slots, three Inspirations, and all your hit points.

It's nearly nine in the morning on Day 13. The assize is a week and a half off, Corvin is in the cellar, and the Heron's Tithe is somewhere in Sedge Creek with a master who now knows you have what he wanted. What does Wren do?

He also gives you something unasked. "He won't strike on open road. He likes water behind him and a narrow way ahead. The Gannet Causeway, half a day out of Saltmarsh on the Marrowford road, where the tide runs both sides. If I wanted a man on a wagon, I'd take him there at the turn of the tide."

Your Insight check is a natural 1, a 4 in all, against his 24. You can't tell if he means it or is steering you into a trap of his own. The advice is useful either way, and it costs nothing to know the place.

  [game] notebook: created an entry for Gannet Causeway; added 2 relationships to Gannet Causeway
DM: Out of the story first, on your two questions. These are my rulings, and I'll keep to them. The Gannet Causeway is about nine hundred paces long. It is passable for about two and a half hours either side of low water and awash at high. The tide turns on a cycle of roughly twelve and a half hours. The next high water after Day 13's nine o'clock peak falls near ten at night on Day 14, and on Day 15 it falls at about twenty to eleven in the morning, with low water near half past four in the afternoon. A crossing on Day 15 would therefore be on the afternoon ebb, with the causeway opening around two.

Now the story. You sleep well, ride out at first light with Kit and Pell's tracker, a wiry, silent woman called Marl Sedge, and reach the flats a little before eleven. You walk the causeway at both tides: first with the sea lapping at its edge near high water, then again as it uncovers through the afternoon.

Your Investigation check is an 18. The causeway is a raised gravel road with timber edging, just wider than a cart, running over salt flats cut by a tidal channel. The landward bank, your side, has a belt of tall reeds and a drainage ditch where eight guards could lie hidden. At the very middle a stone culvert squeezes the road to a single cart-width with water on both sides. A wagon stopped there cannot turn, and it can be blocked from both ends. On the far bank is a drowned boat-shed and a creek mouth where a skiff could lie unseen.

Marl's tracking roll is an 11, which isn't enough to read the banks with confidence. You find what she missed. At the boat-shed there is a freshly cut mooring-stake and trampled reed, perhaps a day old. Someone has been there, and not for fishing.

It's twenty to six in the evening on Day 14, with the flats pink and the water coming back. The wagon leaves Saltmarsh at dawn tomorrow, and the causeway opens on the afternoon ebb. You've three slots, all your Inspiration, Kit, and a tracker. What does Wren do?

Before I leave I place a few bits of dry reed across the mooring stake's path as a tell, so tomorrow I'll know if anyone has used it.

DM: You rise from behind the wheel, wincing, with the staff in your left hand and the rapier low. The words come out steady, and that surprises you. "Take off the hood, sir, or say your real name aloud. Aldous Verrel the younger died at three, and the grave won't speak for you. Pell, hold your guards. Nobody shoots unless he draws."

Your Intimidation check is a 21. The road is quiet. Eight crossbows are on him, the water runs on both sides, and a girl who knows a dead child's grave is standing in front of him. He stands a long moment. Then his hand comes away from the hilt. It goes up slowly to the hood and pushes it back, and the ring turns once on his finger.

He is about fifty, lean and tall, grey in his hair, with an old burn scar along the line of his jaw. He has a long chin and a narrow forehead with a stubborn crease between the brows. You have seen that chin before, on Lord Edric Verrel at Thornhallow. It is not a likeness a man could forge.

"Ansel," he says. "Ansel Verrel, once, before the family paper decided I had never been born." His voice is smooth, unhurried and very tired. "The 'natural son' was a story for hired men. You of all people should forgive a good story." He looks at the lamp-staff, then at your face. "Your mother took the lamp from me, and I have wanted it back for sixteen years. You may put away the speech, child. I'm not going to draw."

He does not draw. His hand stays open at his side, and he does not run. Around you the causeway is very still. Bandit 2 and Bandit 3 sit in the gravel with their hands on their heads. Bandit 1 has slid down the culvert wall and is holding up one empty hand. Sparrow is a dot moving across the mud toward the boat-shed, and no one is near enough to stop him. Corvin sits on the wagon bench with his eyes shut.

It's still round 7 and your turn. Your action is spent, but you still have your bonus action and your movement. You have 3 of 17 hit points, one Bardic Inspiration, and Kit at 3 of 20 beside you. What does Wren do?

DM: You keep your voice low, and that makes the question harder to dodge. "Ansel Verrel, you wore a dead child's grave for a lamp-staff. Tell the guards plainly: did you have Elowen killed?"

Your Insight check is a natural 20. His Deception is a 9. You couldn't miss it if you tried. He looks at the lamp-staff and not at you, and the words come slowly, as if pulled over stones.

"No." His thumb finds the ring and turns it. "I struck her. On the spit, with the squall coming in, when she wouldn't give me the light. She was on her feet when I went down to the boat. I did not stay to see what the weather did, and I didn't learn she was dead until the next year." His mouth works. "You may call that what you like. I have."

There's no lie in it. Beneath the smoothness there's something raw, and for a moment you see what he's carried. Then, as he turns the ring, the band catches the light from the inside, and you see a mark scratched there. It's a small lantern with two letters beside it, E.A., your mother's mark, the one in your sketch. She gave him that ring. She trusted him once, as a patron of her shrines and, you suspect, as more than that. He sees you see it and says nothing.

You turn to Pell. "Bind his hands, gently. He walks to Dame Crowe's assize alive, same as Corvin, and tells it under oath." Pell does it without comment, with two guards beside him. Ansel Verrel offers his wrists as if he'd been waiting sixteen years to be asked.

The fight is over. The causeway is quiet, and the tide is already turning in the channel. The three living bandits are bound where they sat, one dead on the road, one dead at the wagon's tail, and Corvin hasn't moved from the bench. Sparrow is a speck on the far flats, and Marl Sedge is already gone after him through the reeds. You gain 150 XP, so you're at 675 of 900. You have 3 of 17 hit points, no slots, and one Bardic Inspiration. Kit has 3 of 20. It's a little after two in the afternoon, the causeway will stay open for another two hours or so, and you still have a prisoner to carry, a master to explain and a long ride behind you. What does Wren do?

DM: You let out a long breath and sit on the tailboard beside Kit. The sun is warm, and you are the first of the party to realise it. "Pell, Kit's thigh first, then me. Then the prisoners, then the dead on the second horse." Pell is already kneeling with his boot knife and a roll of clean linen. He binds Kit's thigh with hands as steady as a surgeon's, then your ribs, without a word.

Pell answers your question while he works. The nearest dry ground is the rise behind the boat-shed on the far bank, a few hundred paces ahead. The tide has been falling for hours and will hold the causeway open until the early evening, so the wagon can make it with room to spare. Marl Sedge has gone after Sparrow along the reed edge and will report back. The guards carry the two dead bandits to the second horse, and Corvin's wagon rolls on. You make the far bank in ten minutes, and the guards set a watch.

What's established is that the arbiter at Saltmarsh told you to appear at Dame Crowe's assize at Marrowford at month's end, and I ruled that to be the 30th. It names Corvin and the forged leaf, which the arbiter kept as evidence, and your own testimony. It doesn't name Ansel, because nobody knew him then. I also got the date wrong twice. I said "the twentieth" and "a week and a half off," and both were mistakes. The assize is on the 30th, fifteen days from now.

Now the story. You sit down across from Ansel with a cup of water, and he drinks it slowly, both hands around it. Corvin has gone to sleep against the wheel, or is pretending to.

"She gave it to me," Ansel says. "Twenty years ago now, when I was her quiet patron. A younger Verrel with no land of his own paid for her shrines and her road-lanterns, and she sat up with me over the accounts and laughed at my sums." His thumb finds the scratched mark inside the band. "I was closer to her than a patron should be. I asked her to stay on the coast and be mine. She said she was the road's, and wouldn't be anyone's."

He looks at the lamp-staff in your lap. "The lamp on the Gullspit spit was cast from the founding lamp of Thornhallow chapel. My grandsire Aldous gave it to the Lantern-Bearer, and your mother carried it to the sea. When the Verrel paper struck my name, I wanted it back. That's the whole of it, and it's a poor reason for what I did." His voice doesn't change. "She said the lamp answered only to the foot, and the foot answered only to someone who wouldn't sell it."

You can't tell yet how much of that is true. The lamp's origin is something Lord Edric or Brother Osric could confirm, if you ask them. It's ten past three. The causeway is open and Marl hasn't come back. What does Wren do?

Ansel is at the far end of the barn, out of earshot, with his eyes closed.

Corvin answers the second half first, in an easy tone. "The patron is true, child. I saw him once at Elowen's table, a younger Verrel with a ring on his hand. She wore a mark of the same lantern on a chain, and I knew it for her promise to him." He rubs his bound wrists on his knee. "As for dare, Mother Ysolde would have taken the skin off me, and I'm fond of mine."

Your Insight check is a 17, and his Deception is a 13. You can see the seam as plainly as a torn page. His voice stays smooth, but his eyes drop to the lantern flame for half a breath, the way they used to when he trimmed a story.

"That's a half-lie, Corvin," you say quietly. "Which half?"

He studies you a long moment. Then his shoulders drop, just a little. "All right," he says. "Twelve years ago a go-between named H. came to my wagon with coin from a man with a green ring. He wanted me to watch a girl with Elowen's face, and the staff she might one day claim. I sent two vague letters. I kept the coin four years." He looks at his hands. "Then I stopped answering and took up the ledger instead, because it paid better and asked fewer questions. I never gave him your name or the town. I couldn't. I didn't know it myself, then." A dry breath that isn't a laugh. "You wanted to know why I never sold him the lamp. He would never have paid for it. He wanted it for nothing, and he wanted you with it."

DM: You climb into Ember's saddle and Pell shouts "Roll out." The column leaves the burnt barn behind. All morning you ride at the wagon's flank with Kit, and you teach the guards a second line to the road song, a low harmony that drops under the melody like a plough under a hedge. Kit hums it flat and mutters about it. By noon half the guards are doing it right, and the other half are doing it loudly.

You watch the horizon the whole way. Your Perception is a 12, which shows you rooks, a hay-cart and a lone heron lifting from a ditch, and no brown coats or grey sails.

At midday, when the column halts to water the horses, you ride up beside the wagon and ask Ansel quietly, "Lord Edric is your kin by blood. When we reach Thornhallow, do you want me to tell him who you are first, or will you?"

He is slow to answer. His bound hands tighten on the bench rail. "I will," he says. "To his face, before any other voice does. He should hear it from the one who has most to answer for." His Insight is plain to you, a 20 against nothing at all. There's no story in it, only dread and the stubbornness of a man who has finally picked a road. "Then Crowe may do as she likes."

Beside him Corvin stares at the sky and says nothing. For once he has nothing to sell.

The afternoon is long and the road is dull. The wagon rattles through stubble fields and past a ruined mill. At seven in the evening the column reaches a roadside inn, the Plough and Pail. It has a stable, a trestle table in the yard and a landlord who bows to Pell's grey cloak. The guards take the stable loft and the prisoners go in the cellar, with two men on the stair. Tam Rudd sleeps on a pallet under a blanket.

It's seven in the evening on Day 17. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations. Thornhallow is about a day off, and you will see its gatehouse tomorrow afternoon. What does Wren do?

Your Performance comes to a 19. The coins ring into your upturned hat, and you pocket 7 silver and a few coppers by the end, which is a good night for a half-empty inn.

Between songs you ask, as casual as a draught, whether anyone has seen a grey-sailed cutter or a lean man in a brown coat on the river this week. The bargemen shake their heads. A cutter that size doesn't come this far up. A grizzled drover with a pipe taps the stem on his boot.

The doors were barred at dawn, but the churchyard is open to anyone willing to climb. She sets a bolt in her crossbow and says nothing. There's no sound but wind in the yew boughs and, faintly, a rook.

DM: You go down to the yard and meet Pell by the wagon. He looks tired and dusty, with a bandaged hand. You tell him quietly, "Ansel goes up to the study first, with two guards. Corvin waits in the gatehouse under watch until I send for him." Pell nods, and Corvin is taken off to the gatehouse, where he sits with his chin up as if the room were his own.

The study is lit with two lamps. Edric stands behind the desk with Osric at his shoulder and the register open between them. You put your back to the wall by the door with your hand on the lamp-staff. Kit takes the other side. Ansel stands in his chains in the middle of the floor, and he does not look at the book.

"My name is Ansel," he says. "I am the son of Hugh Verrel, Aldous's eldest, and of Joan, a ferryman's daughter at Dunnock. Your uncle did not drown, cousin. He was put on a southern coach at twenty because he would not give her up, and he died of a fever in a hired room before I was born. Your father cut my name from this book when I was ten, because a ferryman's grandson could not stand in the line of the Aldermere blood." His voice is low and steady. "I went by other names. I lost this one and went by Aldous, for the grandsire, because I thought I might wear it until I'd earned the other back. I haven't."

Nobody speaks. Osric's lips are moving. Edric looks at the scraped line, then at the long chin, the same chin as his own, and sits down slowly.

"What did you want?" Edric says at last. "Thornhallow?"

"I wanted to be named," says Ansel. "In the book where I was struck. I wanted the lamp that my grandsire gave and my father's brother lost, and I thought a man who held it could ask. I was wrong about all of it, and I have hurt people who didn't deserve it." He turns his head, just once, and finds your eye. "One of them is standing by your door."

The lamps hiss. Edric's hands are trembling on the desk. He doesn't answer Ansel, and he doesn't look away from him either.

It's twenty-five past six on Day 18. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations. Corvin waits in the gatehouse, Sparrow sleeps in the cellar, and Hale smiles in his locked store. Lord Edric is waiting for someone else to speak first. What does Wren do?

He rises politely when you come in, and his smile is exactly where it always was.

You give him your sweetest grin. "Master Hale, Ansel has just confessed everything to Lord Edric. Your twelve years, the letter from the Marrowford merchant, every coin you took. You were the go-between, and he named you first." You let it sit. "So the only question left is who tells Dame Crowe the rest, you or the man who's already named you. I'll write down whatever you say, word for word."

Your Deception is a 22 against his Insight of 13. The smile doesn't move, but his hand does. It goes to the blanket's edge and smooths it flat, twice, a small tell you've only seen on a man who has just lost his footing. "He would," he says softly. "Of course he would. The master never could keep a clean book." He looks at the lantern. "Write, then. I'd sooner be written by my own hand than his."

He speaks for most of an hour, evenly, and you write it all down on a clerk's paper. The recommendation letter that brought him to Thornhallow came from Ferrand Oake, who keeps a counting-house in Marrowford and places clerks in half the minor houses on the river. Oake's strongroom passed the green-ringed man's coin to him. "Whose coin, I never asked, and I was never told," he says. "Aldous, we called him. Never a face." He doesn't know whether Oake knows who "Aldous" really is. His own cut, the two shillings in the pound, he kept in a tin box under a loose board in his room in the east wing, with every tally in his own hand. "Insurance, mistress. You'd have done the same."

When he finishes, he sits back. "I don't expect mercy. I do expect Dame Crowe to read the tallies." You read the pages back to him, and he nods without comment to each. Kit looks at you over his head.

It's nearly eight on Day 18. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations. You hold Hale's statement. The tin box is in the east wing, Ferrand Oake is in Marrowford, Corvin waits in the gatehouse, and Lord Edric is at supper. What does Wren do?

You open it on the desk with Edric watching every movement. Inside are twelve years of tallies in a neat clerk's hand, with dates, sums and the initials of half the minor houses on the river. Under the tallies lies a single folded letter gone soft at the creases. It is in another hand, signed "for F. Oake", and it is twelve years old.

You read it aloud, because Edric should hear it. "Keep eyes on the girl with Elowen Ashgrove's face, who travels with the storyteller. No hand is to be laid on her until the lamp is found."

Nobody speaks. Edric takes the letter from your hand, reads it himself and sets it down as though it were hot. "This is the man who sent me my steward," he says quietly. "I thanked him with a cask of wine." His jaw tightens. "Seal it. All of it."

You tell him the tin box and the tallies go to Dame Crowe sealed in his name, with Ferrand Oake's name beside them. He drips the green wax himself, presses his ring into it, and signs across the lid. You also ask for the loan of a rider to carry a question to the arbiter at Saltmarsh: has she ever heard of Oake? Edric calls Tobin, and the boy is out of the room before the wax is cool. A rider will ride out at first light on the best horse in the stable. Five days there and five back, but she has the harbour man and the court ledgers, and she may know more than Marrowford does.

It's nearly nine on Day 18. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations. The tin box is sealed, Corvin is in the gatehouse and Ansel is in his guarded room. Marrowford, the assize and Ferrand Oake's counting-house are a day up the road. What does Wren do?

"Whatever you tell me goes down in writing, word for word, and the Warden signs it," you say. He beams. "I'd ask nothing better, mistress."

He talks for an hour. You read his face and his hands the way Corvin taught you. Your Insight is a 19, and it finds nothing to hold on to. His hands lie still on his knees, his voice never rises, and the story hangs together like a good sail. He admits at once that he brokered pedigrees. "It's a trade, mistress, as grain is, and I've been ashamed of it for years." The letter signed for him was his clerk's overreach. Eleven years ago a man who signed himself Aldous wrote to him, wanting a safe go-between to buy news of Elowen Ashgrove's daughter. He sent Hale. He never saw Aldous's face. "Tall, I'm told, and soft-voiced, with a ring. That is all I know of him." The forty marks to Harrow were Aldous's gold, passed by bill at his instruction. The banded chest is his account books. He was taking them, he says, to Dame Crowe himself, because he was afraid. "I'm a coward, mistress. I burned nothing."

You write it down, and Lyle reads it back, and he signs it. The pen shakes only a little. Then you ask him quietly who "Aldous" was to him, as a man. He thinks. "A voice on paper," he says. "A patient one. He paid on the day." His eyes are wet.

It's half past six on Day 20. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations, and 78 gp. You hold Oake's signed statement. Dame Crowe's clerk will come at noon to open the chest, and Pell's wagon with Corvin and Ansel's shadow is still on the road behind. What does Wren do?

Now the story. You let Oake finish signing, then set your record of the quay events on the table and tap the line you copied from his clerk's sheet. "Master Oake, your own tally says you paid Cobb Harrow forty marks to hold the Heron's Tithe at Sedge Creek until the 24th, 'for the lamp.' A bill drawn ahead on the twelfth." It is a good answer, and it is quick. But his hands, which have been lying so still on his knees, stop being still. His left thumb goes to the base of his ring finger, to the place where a ring would sit, and finds nothing there, and his eyes slide to the window and back. You've seen Corvin do exactly that when a story needed one more beat.

"The bill was paid on the seventeenth," you say. "Two days after Aldous was taken on the causeway." You let it lie. "That is a very long standing instruction."

He doesn't answer. For the first time the warmth goes out of his face and something older and flatter looks out. "I think," he says slowly, "I will say no more until Dame Crowe's clerk is here." Warden Lyle, who has been listening with her chin on her fist, nods at the watchman. "That will do for the morning," she says. "He sleeps on it."

DM: Out of the story first, on the handwriting. The old letter in Hale's tin box was signed "for F. Oake", which means a clerk wrote it and signed on Oake's behalf. It is not in Oake's own hand. Comparing it from memory, your Investigation is an 18. The round, looped hand on the old letter matches the clerk's hand on the Harrow tally-sheet, with the same long-tailed F and the same looped g. Oake's own hand on today's statement is upright and cramped, nothing like it. So the clerk wrote both, and the clerk has served Oake for at least twelve years.

Now the story. "Warden, Oake's bill was paid two days after Ansel was taken. Someone is still giving orders, and I'd bet it's Oake himself." Lyle looks at you, then at her keys. She lends you two watchmen and a notary from the bridge-house as witness, and she goes with you herself. Oake's maid lets you in with the clerk's keys.

The strongroom is a small oak-and-iron room behind the office, and it is bare. Your Investigation is a 21. The iron shelves have pale rectangles in the dust where boxes stood a day ago. A drift of wax-flakes lies by the door, from broken seals. In the loading-door lock there are fresh scrapes where a key was forced and not turned. In the alley mud outside runs a hand-cart rut, curving toward the east gate. In the grating of the strongroom floor, caught on a nail, you find a torn corner of a bill stub from Tern & Bellwether, the Saltmarsh bankers. "40 m., 17th, F.O. by his own hand." Oake's own hand drew that bill. The rest of the stub is gone.

The clerk, Corbin Sedley, has not been seen since the afternoon before. His stool is cold, his coat is missing, and his abacus lies on its side. Lyle sets her seal across the strongroom door in green wax. She sends two riders to the east gate to ask about a hand-cart and a lean grey-coated man. The chest, still under the bridge-house seal, holds, you suspect, a great deal of dull grain accounting.

It's twenty past nine on Day 20. You have 17 of 17 hit points, three slots and three Inspirations, and 78 gp. Dame Crowe's clerk arrives at noon to open the chest. Crake and the clerk are gone with whatever was in the strongroom, and Pell's wagon will be at the gates by dusk. What does Wren do?

DM: Faerie Fire's gone, and Wren's in a bad spot. Here is where things stand.

You called out to Crake, and then your spell caught only one man. The violet light clung to a bandit, but the other two shrugged it off, one of them with room to spare. Kit moved up to the gate rail and put a bolt in the lit man's thigh. He's reeling, but he dived at the ditch anyway and his scimitar bit you for 7. The pain knocked the song out of the spell, and the glow went out. His mate came down on you next and missed by a hand.

Now two bandits stand on the bank above you. One is hurt badly and clutching his thigh, the other is fresh. Crake is coming across the yard behind them with his club, unhurried and not yet in reach. The loft crossbow just missed you. Kit is forty feet or so up the hedge with her crossbow, and the skiff is drifting slowly out into the pond.

You have 10 of 17 hit points and two 1st-level slots, three Inspirations, a rapier and a dagger. What does Wren do?
